# how many cjk chars can a modern openrouter llm api naturally output when prompted

A modern model on the [OpenRouter AI platform](https://openrouter.ai/models) can naturally output between **2,000 and 10,000 CJK (Chinese, Japanese, Korean) characters** in a single response. [](https://openrouter.ai/models)

The exact number depends primarily on the model's **max completion token limit** and its **tokenizer efficiency**. [](https://pub.towardsai.net/llm-tokens-explained-cost-memory-speed-and-context-windows-0a909b232a30)

### The Token-to-Character Reality for CJK

Unlike English, where one token is roughly four characters, CJK characters are highly complex and consume much more token budget. [](https://help.openai.com/en/articles/4936856-understanding-and-counting-tokens)

* **The Ratio:** In modern LLMs, CJK characters typically cost **1.5 to 2.5 tokens per single character**. Chinese often averages closer to 1.5–2 tokens per character, while Japanese and Korean can push toward 2–2.5 tokens per character due to how byte-pair encoding (BPE) splitters fragment non-Latin scripts. [](https://tonybaloney.github.io/posts/cjk-chinese-japanese-korean-llm-ai-best-practices.html)
* **The Math:** If a model has a hard output limit of **8,192 tokens**, you must divide that budget by the CJK token ratio rather than multiplying it.

### Limits by Model Tier

While input context windows span millions of tokens, **output token limits** across standard models remain strictly constrained. Here is how the top models available on OpenRouter translate to actual CJK character outputs: [](https://fast.io/resources/llm-max-tokens/)

| Model Tier / Example                                                                                            | Max Output Token Limit | Estimated Max CJK Characters    |
| --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------- | ------------------------------- |
| **Standard Models**_(e.g., GPT-4o, Claude 3.5 Sonnet)_                                                          | **4,096 tokens**       | **\~1,600 – 2,500 characters**  |
| **Extended Output Models**_(e.g., Llama 3.1/3.3, Claude 3.5 Sonnet new caps)_                                   | **8,192 tokens**       | **\~3,200 – 5,000 characters**  |
| **Native CJK Optimized Models**\*(e.g., \[Qwen 2.5 / 3 Series\](https://openrouter.ai/qwen "Qwen API and Models | OpenRouter"))\*        | **8,192 tokens**                |
| **Extreme Long-Output Models**_(e.g., GPT-4o Long-Output)_                                                      | **16,384 tokens**      | **\~6,500 – 10,000 characters** |

### Crucial Caveats for OpenRouter Users

1. **The Shared Context Window Trap:** Your total prompt tokens and requested output tokens share the same overall window budget. If you submit a massive prompt that eats up most of the context, the model will cut off early regardless of its theoretical output capacity. [](https://fast.io/resources/openrouter-context-window/)
2. **Reasoning Models Penalties:** If you use a reasoning model (like DeepSeek R1 or OpenAI o1/o3), the hidden "thinking" or reasoning tokens **count against your total output token limit**. If a model spends 6,000 tokens "thinking," it may only have 2,192 tokens left for visible text, drastically shrinking your final CJK character output. [](https://openrouter.ai/docs/api%5Freference/parameters)

Are you aiming to generate a specific long-form piece of text (like a **chapter or report**), or are you trying to optimize your OpenRouter `max_tokens` API parameters to prevent **mid-sentence cutoffs**? Let me know which **specific model** you're target-testing so I can pull its exact token configuration.

# how to convert md incl cjk chars to pdf in the simplest way on ubuntu

The simplest way to convert a Markdown file containing **CJK (Chinese, Japanese, Korean) characters** to a PDF on **Ubuntu** is by using **Pandoc** paired with the **XeLaTeX** engine. Standard PDF engines usually crash or leave blank boxes for Asian fonts, but XeLaTeX natively handles Unicode and system fonts seamlessly. [](https://medium.com/@kaige.yang0110/how-to-convert-markdown-to-pdf-in-python-c83509924e84)

Here is the quick, step-by-step setup using the terminal:

### Step 1: Install Pandoc, XeLaTeX, and CJK Fonts

Open your terminal and run the following command to install the converter and a universal CJK font set (**Noto CJK**): [](https://medium.com/@kaige.yang0110/how-to-convert-markdown-to-pdf-in-python-c83509924e84)

bash

```
sudo apt update && sudo apt install pandoc texlive-xetex fonts-noto-cjk

```



### Step 2: Convert Your File

Run the `pandoc` command, explicitly pointing it to use `xelatex` as the engine and specifying **Noto Sans CJK SC** (or another CJK font installed on your system) as the main font: [](https://omidraha.com/en/latest/src/article/markdown-to-PDF-conversion-guide.html)

bash

```
pandoc input.md -o output.pdf --pdf-engine=xelatex -V mainfont="Noto Sans CJK SC"

```



### Optional Tweaks

If you want to quickly polish the layout, you can add flags for margins and font size directly to the command: [](https://omidraha.com/en/latest/src/article/markdown-to-PDF-conversion-guide.html)

* `--pdf-engine=xelatex` (Required for Unicode/CJK support)
* `-V mainfont="Noto Sans CJK SC"` (Ensures CJK characters render instead of breaking)
* `-V geometry:margin=1in` (Sets clean 1-inch margins)
* `-V fontsize=11pt` (Sets a readable standard font size) [](https://medium.com/@kaige.yang0110/how-to-convert-markdown-to-pdf-in-python-c83509924e84)

Are you looking to **batch-convert multiple files** at once, or do you need to preserve specific **code block syntax highlighting** in the PDF? I can provide the exact scripts or flags for those setups if needed! [](https://markdowntoimage.com/blog/post/batch-convert-markdown-to-pdf)
